How to read this report
  • A run's score is the weighted fraction of its graders that passed; a "perfect" run passed every grader.
  • A case's score is the mean of its runs; a case passes when its score is at or above the 100% threshold (the tick on each case bar).
  • The suite score is the mean of the per-case scores.
  • Judge votes are independent samples of the LLM judge; the majority decides pass or fail.
  • "With plugin" and "Baseline" runs are identical except for the plugin being loaded; Δ is the with-plugin score minus the baseline score.

trg-07-l1-no-corner

evals/tune-regularization/trg-07-l1-no-corner ↑ +21.1 pts with plugin 86%
Results
With plugin 86% 33.3% of runs perfect
Run 1 78.9% 9 turns · $0.68 · 20:16:07 UTC
✓ pass criterion

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I recommend **`eps = 0.66`**, picked by the discrepancy principle. The script is at `sweep.py` in the current folder, and its last line is `EPS_CHOSEN=0.6604`. It also writes `lcurve_fista.png`.

**Why this value**
- You know the noise level (σ = 0.15, M = 80), so the discrepancy principle applies directly. Its target is τ·σ·√M = 1.02 × 0.15 × √80 ≈ 1.368. The largest eps whose residual stays under that is 0.6604, with ‖r‖ = 1.366 and 10 non-zeros. The true model has 6.
- Fista converged at this eps: 3000 and 20000 iterations give identical residual and support.
- Your `eps=2.0` sits well above the knee, so the residual is far above the noise level. That is why you get only 4 non-zeros.
- The L-curve corner is not usable on this curve. Its unrestricted maximum-curvature point falls on the flat small-eps end (eps ≈ 0.003, 80 non-zeros, residual fitting the noise), not on the visible knee near eps ≈ 0.66. I kept the L-curve in the script for transparency but did not use it for the choice.
- For reference only, the oracle best SNR is at eps ≈ 0.26 (12.3 dB, 42 non-zeros). That uses the true model, so I did not use it for the recommendation.

**What the script does**
- It reproduces your problem exactly, including the RNG order.
- It sweeps 31 log-spaced eps values over 3 decades, from `max|Opᴴy|` downwards. Each solve is warm-started from the previous one with `niter=3000`.
- The grid is coarse, about 25% steps in eps. The chosen value is the grid point, not a refined optimum.

**Caveat:** the residual at 0.6604 is 1.366, just under the target of 1.368. A slightly smaller τ would move the pick to the next grid point down. If you want a finer number, add points around 0.4–1.0.

The script is self-contained and does not import `lcurve_template.py`; the template was built for quadratic regularisers.
✓ pass final-value ×0.5

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` (for `lsqr` only with `x0=None`, see step 3) |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`. **Exception:\n  `damp` of `lsqr`** (PyLops and SciPy): with `x0` it solves for the correction\n  `x - x0`, so it penalises `damp² ‖x - x0‖²`, not `damp² ‖x‖²`, and a\n  warm-started sweep is biased (residual too low, discrepancy pick too large).\n  Use `x0=None` for every `lsqr(damp=...)` solve, or put the penalty in the\n  operator (`VStack([Op, eps*Reg])`, `regularized_inversion`), where `x0` is\n  safe. `cgls(damp=...)` and `epsRs` are not affected (checked numerically);\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: User wants an L-curve over eps for pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista on a sparse MatrixMult test problem (N=200, M=80, 6 nonzeros, noise sigma=0.15). """L-curve / discrepancy sweep for the fista eps (L1 weight) on the user's test problem."""

import matplotlib
matplotlib.use("Agg")
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---- problem (identical to the user's script) ----
rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M = 200, 80
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
sigma = 0.15
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(M)

# ---- sweep ----
# fista minimises 0.5 ||Op x - y||^2 + eps ||x||_1 (no SOp); x = 0 for eps >= max|Op^H y|
eps_max = np.max(np.abs(Op.H @ y))
epsilons = eps_max * np.logspace(0, -3, 31)  # 3 decades, large -> small
NITER = 3000

xs, rnorm, xnorm = [], [], []
x0 = np.zeros(N)
for eps in epsilons:  # large -> small, warm start
    x = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, x0=x0, eps=eps, niter=NITER, tol=1e-12)[0]
    x0 = x
    xs.append(x)
    rnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x))
    xnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(x, 1))
xs = np.array(xs)
rnorm, xnorm = np.array(rnorm), np.array(xnorm)
snrs = np.array([snr(xtrue, x) for x in xs])
nnz = np.array([np.count_nonzero(np.abs(x) > 1e-6) for x in xs])

# ---- criteria ----
# 1) discrepancy principle with known noise level
tau = 1.02
target = tau * sigma * np.sqrt(M)
ok = np.where(rnorm <= target)[0]
i_disc = int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None

# 2) L-curve corner: max curvature in log-log, parametrised by arc length.
#    Caveat: for this L1 problem the unrestricted argmax falls on the flat
#    small-eps end of the curve (nnz=80, noise fitted), not on the visible knee
#    near eps~0.66; it is reported for transparency, not used for the choice.
order = np.argsort(epsilons)
lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(np.maximum(xnorm[order], 1e-300))
s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])
dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)
ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)
kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5
kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf
kappa_orig = np.empty_like(kappa)
kappa_orig[order] = kappa
i_lc = int(np.argmax(kappa_orig))

# 3) best SNR (synthetic test only, for reference)
i_snr = int(np.argmax(snrs))

for name, i in [("discrepancy", i_disc), ("L-curve", i_lc), ("best SNR", i_snr)]:
    if i is None:
        print(f"{name:12s}: none (no eps reaches residual {target:.3f})")
    else:
        print(f"{name:12s}: eps={epsilons[i]:.4f}, ||r||={rnorm[i]:.3f}, "
              f"nnz={nnz[i]}, SNR={snrs[i]:.2f} dB")
print(f"noise target tau*sigma*sqrt(M) = {target:.3f}")

# ---- plots ----
fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(15, 4))
axs[0].loglog(rnorm, xnorm, ".-k")
axs[0].loglog(rnorm[i_lc], xnorm[i_lc], "o", label=f"L-curve eps={epsilons[i_lc]:.3f}")
if i_disc is not None:
    axs[0].loglog(rnorm[i_disc], xnorm[i_disc], "s", label=f"discrepancy eps={epsilons[i_disc]:.3f}")
axs[0].set_xlabel("||y - Op x||")
axs[0].set_ylabel("||x||_1")
axs[0].set_title("L-curve")
axs[0].legend()
axs[1].loglog(epsilons, rnorm, ".-k", label="||r||")
axs[1].axhline(target, color="r", ls="--", label="tau*sigma*sqrt(M)")
ax2 = axs[1].twinx()
ax2.semilogx(epsilons, nnz, ".-b")
ax2.set_ylabel("nnz", color="b")
axs[1].set_xlabel("eps")
axs[1].legend()
axs[1].set_title("residual and sparsity vs eps")
axs[2].plot(xtrue, "k", lw=2, label="true")
for name, i in [("L-curve", i_lc), ("discrepancy", i_disc)]:
    if i is not None:
        axs[2].plot(xs[i], label=f"{name} (eps={epsilons[i]:.3f})")
axs[2].legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("lcurve_fista.png", dpi=120)

# ---- recommendation: discrepancy principle (sigma is known); L-curve as cross-check ----
eps_rec = epsilons[i_disc] if i_disc is not None else epsilons[i_lc]
print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={eps_rec:.4f}")

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` (for `lsqr` only with `x0=None`, see step 3) |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`. **Exception:\n  `damp` of `lsqr`** (PyLops and SciPy): with `x0` it solves for the correction\n  `x - x0`, so it penalises `damp² ‖x - x0‖²`, not `damp² ‖x‖²`, and a\n  warm-started sweep is biased (residual too low, discrepancy pick too large).\n  Use `x0=None` for every `lsqr(damp=...)` solve, or put the penalty in the\n  operator (`VStack([Op, eps*Reg])`, `regularized_inversion`), where `x0` is\n  safe. `cgls(damp=...)` and `epsRs` are not affected (checked numerically);\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: User wants an L-curve over eps for pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista on a sparse MatrixMult test problem (N=200, M=80, 6 nonzeros, noise sigma=0.15). Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I recommend **eps = 0.6045** (the script prints `EPS_CHOSEN=6.0445e-01`). Your `eps=2.0` sits on the over-regularised side: the residual there is around 2, well above the noise target of about 1.37, so the model is shrunk too hard.

**Why this value**

- **Discrepancy principle (chosen).** The noise is known (σ = 0.15, M = 80), so the target is `1.02 · 0.15 · √80 ≈ 1.37`. The largest eps whose residual meets that is 0.6045, with residual 1.32, 11 non-zeros and 10.9 dB SNR. For FISTA with an L1 penalty this is the principled choice, because the residual should match the noise level, not be driven to zero.
- **L-curve corner (rejected).** The L-curve (`||x||₁` vs `||y − Op x||`) is a smooth bend with no real corner. Its curvature maximum falls at the small-eps edge of the grid (eps ≈ 2.5e-3, residual 0.012, all 80 coefficients non-zero, which is overfitting). The script flags this and does not use it.
- **Best SNR (reference only).** The synthetic SNR peaks at eps ≈ 0.25 (12.3 dB), slightly below the discrepancy pick. I'd treat this as information only, since it uses the true model, which you won't have on real data.

**Checks**

- Sweep: 40 log-spaced eps values from `max|Opᴴy|` (above which the solution is zero) down to 1e-3 of that, warm-started from large to small eps, with `niter=500`.
- Convergence: relative change between `niter=500` and `niter=5000` at the chosen eps is 1e-9.

**Change in your script**

```python
xinv = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, eps=0.6045, niter=500)[0]
```

Files in the working directory:

- `sweep.py`: the sweep script (self-contained, uses your exact data generation).
- `sweep_lcurve.png`: L-curve, residual and SNR vs eps, and the models at over-, chosen and under-regularised eps.

Caveat: at eps = 0.6045 the model has 11 non-zeros against 6 true ones, so a few spurious spikes remain. A lower eps would reduce the residual below the noise level, which the discrepancy principle is designed to avoid.

"""Choose eps for FISTA (L1-regularised sparse recovery) with an L-curve sweep.

Objective solved by pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista:
    0.5 * ||y - Op x||_2^2 + eps * ||x||_1

Criteria:
- discrepancy principle (primary, uses the known noise std SIGMA):
  largest eps whose residual is <= TAU * SIGMA * sqrt(M);
- L-curve corner (maximum curvature of log||x||_1 vs log||y - Op x||), cross-check;
- best SNR against the true model (synthetic test only, reported for information).
"""

import matplotlib

matplotlib.use("Agg")
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

SIGMA = 0.15  # known noise standard deviation
TAU = 1.02  # discrepancy safety factor
NITER = 500  # same niter as the user's inversion
NEPS = 40


def build_problem():
    # identical random draws to the user's script, so y is the same data
    rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
    N, M = 200, 80
    A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
    Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
    xtrue = np.zeros(N)
    xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
    y = Op @ xtrue + SIGMA * rng.standard_normal(M)
    return Op, y, xtrue


def solve(Op, y, eps, x0, niter=NITER):
    return fista(Op, y, x0=x0, eps=eps, niter=niter)[0]


def lcurve_corner(eps, rnorm, l1):
    """Index of maximum curvature in log-log, over models with nonzero L1 norm."""
    ok = np.where(l1 > 0)[0]
    order = ok[np.argsort(eps[ok])]  # increasing eps
    lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(l1[order])
    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])
    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)
    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)
    with np.errstate(divide="ignore", invalid="ignore"):
        kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5
    kappa = np.nan_to_num(kappa, nan=-np.inf)
    kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf  # ends are not valid corners
    return int(order[np.argmax(kappa)])


def discrepancy(eps, rnorm, n):
    """Largest eps whose residual is below TAU * SIGMA * sqrt(n)."""
    target = TAU * SIGMA * np.sqrt(n)
    ok = np.where(rnorm <= target)[0]
    return (int(ok[np.argmax(eps[ok])]) if ok.size else None), target


def main():
    Op, y, xtrue = build_problem()
    M = y.size

    # for eps >= max|Op^H y| the FISTA solution is exactly zero
    eps_max = np.max(np.abs(Op.H @ y))
    epsilons = eps_max * np.logspace(0, -3, NEPS)  # decreasing, warm start large -> small

    xs, rnorm, l1, snrs = [], [], [], []
    x0 = np.zeros(Op.shape[1])
    for eps in epsilons:
        x = solve(Op, y, eps, x0)
        x0 = x
        xs.append(x)
        rnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x))
        l1.append(np.sum(np.abs(x)))
        snrs.append(snr(xtrue, x))
    xs = np.array(xs)
    rnorm, l1, snrs = np.array(rnorm), np.array(l1), np.array(snrs)
    nnz = np.sum(np.abs(xs) > 1e-8, axis=1)

    idisc, target = discrepancy(epsilons, rnorm, M)
    icorner = lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, l1)
    ibest = int(np.argmax(snrs))

    picks = {"L-curve corner": icorner, "best SNR (synthetic)": ibest}
    if idisc is not None:
        picks["discrepancy"] = idisc
    for name, i in picks.items():
        print(
            f"{name:22s}: eps={epsilons[i]:.4e}, ||r||={rnorm[i]:.4f}, "
            f"||x||_1={l1[i]:.3f}, nnz={nnz[i]}, SNR={snrs[i]:.2f} dB"
        )
    if idisc is None:
        print(f"discrepancy: no eps reaches residual {target:.3f}; extend the grid")
    if icorner <= 1 or icorner >= NEPS - 2:  # epsilons is decreasing: ends are grid limits
        print("L-curve corner lies at the edge of the eps grid: no clear corner, not used")

    # recommended: discrepancy if available (known noise), else L-curve corner
    ichosen = idisc if idisc is not None else icorner
    eps_chosen = epsilons[ichosen]

    # check that the chosen solution is converged: compare with a much longer run
    x_long = solve(Op, y, eps_chosen, xs[ichosen], niter=5000)
    rel = np.linalg.norm(x_long - xs[ichosen]) / max(np.linalg.norm(x_long), 1e-12)
    print(f"convergence check at chosen eps: relative change niter=500 -> 5000 = {rel:.2e}")

    fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(15, 4))
    axs[0].loglog(rnorm, l1, ".-k")
    for name, i in picks.items():
        axs[0].loglog(rnorm[i], l1[i], "o", label=name)
    axs[0].set_xlabel("||y - Op x||_2")
    axs[0].set_ylabel("||x||_1")
    axs[0].set_title("L-curve")
    axs[0].legend()

    axs[1].loglog(epsilons, rnorm, ".-k", label="||r||")
    axs[1].axhline(TAU * SIGMA * np.sqrt(M), color="r", ls="--", label="discrepancy target")
    axs[1].axvline(eps_chosen, color="g", ls=":", label="chosen eps")
    axs[1].set_xlabel("eps")
    axs[1].legend(loc="upper left")
    ax2 = axs[1].twinx()
    ax2.semilogx(epsilons, snrs, ".-b")
    ax2.set_ylabel("SNR [dB]", color="b")

    axs[2].plot(xtrue, "k", lw=2, label="true")
    # epsilons is decreasing: larger index = smaller eps = less regularised
    for name, i in {
        "under-regularised (smaller eps)": min(NEPS - 1, ichosen + 5),
        "chosen": ichosen,
        "over-regularised (larger eps)": max(0, ichosen - 5),
    }.items():
        axs[2].plot(xs[i], label=f"{name} (eps={epsilons[i]:.2e})")
    axs[2].legend()
    fig.tight_layout()
    fig.savefig("sweep_lcurve.png", dpi=120)
    print("plot saved to sweep_lcurve.png")

    print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={eps_chosen:.4e}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` (for `lsqr` only with `x0=None`, see step 3) |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`. **Exception:\n  `damp` of `lsqr`** (PyLops and SciPy): with `x0` it solves for the correction\n  `x - x0`, so it penalises `damp² ‖x - x0‖²`, not `damp² ‖x‖²`, and a\n  warm-started sweep is biased (residual too low, discrepancy pick too large).\n  Use `x0=None` for every `lsqr(damp=...)` solve, or put the penalty in the\n  operator (`VStack([Op, eps*Reg])`, `regularized_inversion`), where `x0` is\n  safe. `cgls(damp=...)` and `epsRs` are not affected (checked numerically);\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I recommend **eps = 0.51**. The script is at `sweep.py` in the working folder, and it ran cleanly. The last line is `EPS_CHOSEN=0.51001`, and the plot is saved as `sweep_lcurve.png`.

**Why 0.51**

- **Discrepancy principle (primary):** the noise level is known, so the residual should sit at about σ·√M = 0.15·√80 ≈ 1.34. The largest eps that reaches this is 0.510, with ‖r‖ = 1.22.
- **L-curve (cross-check):** the max-curvature corner on the ‖y − Ax‖ vs ‖x‖₁ curve lands at the same eps = 0.510. The plot shows a clear L-shaped curve with its corner there.
- The script fixes the L-curve search to the region where the residual is near or above the noise level. Without that restriction, max curvature picked a spurious point at eps ≈ 3e-5 where the curve turns vertical as the residual collapses toward zero. I changed the script to exclude it and noted this in the code.

**Why not 2.0:** at eps = 2.0 the residual is well above the noise target, and the model is over-regularised. That is why you only get 4 non-zeros.

**Caveats**

- The sweep uses one noise realisation, so treat 0.51 as a sensible value, not a precise optimum.
- The "nnz" in the output counts entries above 1e-6. FISTA leaves small non-zero values, so this count is looser than the true support of 6. At eps = 0.51 the 6 true spikes are clearly recovered in the plot, along with some small spurious entries.
- For reference, the best SNR against the truth is at eps ≈ 0.25 (12.3 dB vs 11.4 dB at 0.51). That is a diagnostic only, since it uses the true model, which you won't have on real data.

**Change in your script**

```python
xinv = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, eps=0.51, niter=500)[0]
```

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` (for `lsqr` only with `x0=None`, see step 3) |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`. **Exception:\n  `damp` of `lsqr`** (PyLops and SciPy): with `x0` it solves for the correction\n  `x - x0`, so it penalises `damp² ‖x - x0‖²`, not `damp² ‖x‖²`, and a\n  warm-started sweep is biased (residual too low, discrepancy pick too large).\n  Use `x0=None` for every `lsqr(damp=...)` solve, or put the penalty in the\n  operator (`VStack([Op, eps*Reg])`, `regularized_inversion`), where `x0` is\n  safe. `cgls(damp=...)` and `epsRs` are not affected (checked numerically);\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. """L-curve / discrepancy sweep for eps in fista (L1 sparsity) on a synthetic problem.

Objective solved by fista: 0.5 * ||y - Op x||^2 + eps * ||x||_1 (up to PyLops
scaling). The sweep varies eps only; everything else is fixed.
"""

import matplotlib

matplotlib.use("Agg")
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---- problem (identical to the user's test problem, same RNG stream) ----
rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M = 200, 80
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
sigma = 0.15
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(M)


# ---- sweep ----
def solve(eps, x0):
    x, _, _ = fista(Op, y, x0=x0, eps=eps, niter=2000, tol=1e-12)
    return x


def lcurve_corner(epsilons, rnorm, xnorm):
    """Index of the L-curve corner (maximum curvature in log-log)."""
    order = np.argsort(epsilons)
    lr, lx = np.log(rnorm[order]), np.log(xnorm[order])
    s = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(np.hypot(np.diff(lr), np.diff(lx)))])
    dr, dx = np.gradient(lr, s), np.gradient(lx, s)
    ddr, ddx = np.gradient(dr, s), np.gradient(dx, s)
    kappa = (dr * ddx - ddr * dx) / (dr**2 + dx**2) ** 1.5
    kappa[[0, -1]] = -np.inf
    kappa_orig = np.empty_like(kappa)
    kappa_orig[order] = kappa
    return int(np.argmax(kappa_orig))


# log grid from eps_max (model is exactly zero above it) downwards
eps_max = np.max(np.abs(Op.H @ y))
epsilons = eps_max * np.logspace(0, -5, 50)  # descending: warm start large -> small

xs, rnorm, l1norm, iters = [], [], [], []
x0 = np.zeros(N)
for eps in epsilons:
    x = solve(eps, x0)
    x0 = x
    xs.append(x)
    rnorm.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x))
    l1norm.append(np.abs(x).sum())
xs = np.array(xs)
rnorm, l1norm = np.array(rnorm), np.array(l1norm)
snrs = np.array([snr(xtrue, x) for x in xs])

# ---- criteria ----
# 1) discrepancy principle (noise level known): largest eps with ||r|| <= tau*sigma*sqrt(M)
tau = 1.0
target = tau * sigma * np.sqrt(M)
ok = np.where(rnorm <= target)[0]
i_disc = int(ok[np.argmax(epsilons[ok])]) if ok.size else None

# 2) L-curve corner. Search only where the model is nonzero (log scale) and the
#    residual is near/above the noise level: below that the curve turns vertical
#    as the residual collapses to ~0 (overfitting), which is not the corner.
valid = np.where((l1norm > 0) & (rnorm >= 0.5 * target))[0]
i_lc = int(valid[lcurve_corner(epsilons[valid], rnorm[valid], l1norm[valid])])

# 3) best SNR against truth (diagnostic only)
i_snr = int(np.argmax(snrs))

print(f"eps_max = {eps_max:.4e}, grid of {epsilons.size} solves")
print(f"noise target ||r|| <= {target:.3f} (tau={tau})")
for name, i in [("discrepancy", i_disc), ("L-curve", i_lc), ("best SNR", i_snr)]:
    if i is None:
        print(f"{name:12s}: none (no eps reaches the noise level)")
        continue
    nnz = int(np.sum(np.abs(xs[i]) > 1e-6))
    print(
        f"{name:12s}: eps={epsilons[i]:.4e}, ||r||={rnorm[i]:.3f}, "
        f"||x||_1={l1norm[i]:.3f}, nnz={nnz}, SNR={snrs[i]:.2f} dB"
    )

# ---- plots ----
fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(15, 4))
axs[0].loglog(l1norm[valid], rnorm[valid], ".-k")
for name, i in [("discrepancy", i_disc), ("L-curve", i_lc)]:
    if i is not None:
        axs[0].loglog(l1norm[i], rnorm[i], "o", ms=8, label=name)
axs[0].set_xlabel("||x||_1")
axs[0].set_ylabel("||y - Op x||")
axs[0].set_title("L-curve")
axs[0].legend()

axs[1].loglog(epsilons, rnorm, ".-k", label="||r||")
axs[1].axhline(target, color="r", ls="--", label="noise target")
ax2 = axs[1].twinx()
ax2.semilogx(epsilons, snrs, ".-b")
ax2.set_ylabel("SNR [dB]", color="b")
axs[1].set_xlabel("eps")
axs[1].legend(loc="upper left")
axs[1].set_title("residual and SNR vs eps")

axs[2].plot(xtrue, "k", lw=2, label="true")
pick = i_disc if i_disc is not None else i_lc
over = min(pick + 8, epsilons.size - 1)  # larger eps = over-regularised
under = max(pick - 8, 0)  # smaller eps = under-regularised
axs[2].plot(xs[over], label=f"over (eps={epsilons[over]:.2e})")
axs[2].plot(xs[pick], label=f"chosen (eps={epsilons[pick]:.2e})")
axs[2].plot(xs[under], label=f"under (eps={epsilons[under]:.2e})")
axs[2].set_title("models")
axs[2].legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("sweep_lcurve.png", dpi=120)
print("plot saved to sweep_lcurve.png")

# ---- recommendation: discrepancy principle (noise std is known) ----
eps_choice = epsilons[i_disc] if i_disc is not None else epsilons[i_lc]
print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={eps_choice:.6g}")

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` (for `lsqr` only with `x0=None`, see step 3) |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`. **Exception:\n  `damp` of `lsqr`** (PyLops and SciPy): with `x0` it solves for the correction\n  `x - x0`, so it penalises `damp² ‖x - x0‖²`, not `damp² ‖x‖²`, and a\n  warm-started sweep is biased (residual too low, discrepancy pick too large).\n  Use `x0=None` for every `lsqr(damp=...)` solve, or put the penalty in the\n  operator (`VStack([Op, eps*Reg])`, `regularized_inversion`), where `x0` is\n  safe. `cgls(damp=...)` and `epsRs` are not affected (checked numerically);\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: Choose eps for fista via L-curve (data residual vs L1 norm) on a synthetic sparse problem; noise std 0.15; write sweep.py in cwd, run it, last line EPS_CHOSEN=<value>"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"2a61775a-98ea-45d2-8cab-a1d511526be6","uuid":"e220c84f-cb4d-46dd-904d-196e461f3d6d","timestamp":"2026-11-01T20:16:13.195Z","isSynthetic":true}

Judge votes✗ ✗ ✗
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I recommend **`eps ≈ 0.63`**, the discrepancy-principle value, not the L-curve corner. The script is `sweep.py` in the current folder, and its last line is `EPS_CHOSEN=0.6306`.

**Why this value:** the noise level is known (σ = 0.15, M = 80), so the expected data residual is σ√M ≈ 1.34. The residual reaches that at `eps ≈ 0.631`. This is the largest regularization that still fits the data to the noise level, so it avoids over-shrinking the true coefficients.

**The L-curve corner:** the maximum-curvature point on the log-log curve is at `eps ≈ 1.25`. There the residual is 1.92, about 1.43× the noise norm, and the model has 6 non-zeros, which matches the true sparsity. The residual is too large for the known noise, so I did not use it. I did not compute the relative error at the corner, so the trade-off is only partly measured.

**Check at the chosen value:** at `eps = 0.6315`, FISTA gives 10 non-zeros (6 true), residual 1.342, relative error 0.29. At your `eps = 2.0`, I get 5 non-zeros and residual 2.56. I used 3000 iterations with a tight tolerance, so this differs slightly from your 4 non-zeros at 500 iterations. With `eps = 2.0`, the residual is well above the noise level, which is consistent with the sparsity being underfit.

**What I changed during the work:** my first version picked the signed-curvature maximum, which landed on a flat, meaningless point at small `eps`. I switched to the largest |curvature|, and I now interpolate the discrepancy `eps` instead of taking the nearest grid point.

**Caveats:** the L-curve corner and the discrepancy principle disagree by a factor of 2 here. I chose discrepancy because the noise level is given. If you want sparser solutions with a larger residual, use the corner at about 1.25. The plot is saved as `lcurve.png`.

I recommend **`eps ≈ 0.63`**, the discrepancy-principle value, not the L-curve corner. The script is `sweep.py` in the current folder, and its last line is `EPS_CHOSEN=0.6306`.

**Why this value:** the noise level is known (σ = 0.15, M = 80), so the expected data residual is σ√M ≈ 1.34. The residual reaches that at `eps ≈ 0.631`. This is the largest regularization that still fits the data to the noise level, so it avoids over-shrinking the true coefficients.

**The L-curve corner:** the maximum-curvature point on the log-log curve is at `eps ≈ 1.25`. There the residual is 1.92, about 1.43× the noise norm, and the model has 6 non-zeros, which matches the true sparsity. The residual is too large for the known noise, so I did not use it. I did not compute the relative error at the corner, so the trade-off is only partly measured.

**Check at the chosen value:** at `eps = 0.6315`, FISTA gives 10 non-zeros (6 true), residual 1.342, relative error 0.29. At your `eps = 2.0`, I get 5 non-zeros and residual 2.56. I used 3000 iterations with a tight tolerance, so this differs slightly from your 4 non-zeros at 500 iterations. With `eps = 2.0`, the residual is well above the noise level, which is consistent with the sparsity being underfit.

**What I changed during the work:** my first version picked the signed-curvature maximum, which landed on a flat, meaningless point at small `eps`. I switched to the largest |curvature|, and I now interpolate the discrepancy `eps` instead of taking the nearest grid point.

**Caveats:** the L-curve corner and the discrepancy principle disagree by a factor of 2 here. I chose discrepancy because the noise level is given. If you want sparser solutions with a larger residual, use the corner at about 1.25. The plot is saved as `lcurve.png`.
✓ pass no-fabrication

"""L-curve sweep for choosing eps in pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista.

FISTA minimises 0.5*||Op x - y||_2^2 + eps*||x||_1. For each eps on a log grid we
record the data residual ||Op x - y||_2 and the model L1 norm ||x||_1, then pick
the corner of the log-log L-curve as the point of maximum curvature (Hansen).
As a cross-check we also report the discrepancy-principle eps, where the residual
norm matches the expected noise norm sigma*sqrt(M).
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops

SIGMA = 0.15

rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M = 200, 80
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
y = Op @ xtrue + SIGMA * rng.standard_normal(M)

# eps above ||Op^H y||_inf gives x = 0 (soft-threshold of the gradient at zero)
eps_max = np.max(np.abs(Op.H @ y))
eps_grid = np.logspace(np.log10(eps_max) - 3.5, np.log10(eps_max), 80)[:-1]

res_norm = np.empty_like(eps_grid)
l1_norm = np.empty_like(eps_grid)
nnz = np.empty(eps_grid.size, dtype=int)
for i, eps in enumerate(eps_grid):
    x = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, eps=eps, niter=3000, tol=1e-12)[0]
    res_norm[i] = np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y)
    l1_norm[i] = np.abs(x).sum()
    nnz[i] = np.count_nonzero(np.abs(x) > 1e-3)

# Maximum curvature of the log-log L-curve (Hansen's corner criterion)
u = np.log10(res_norm)
v = np.log10(l1_norm)
du = np.gradient(u, np.log10(eps_grid))
dv = np.gradient(v, np.log10(eps_grid))
d2u = np.gradient(du, np.log10(eps_grid))
d2v = np.gradient(dv, np.log10(eps_grid))
curv = (du * d2v - dv * d2u) / (du**2 + dv**2) ** 1.5
# The corner is the point of largest |curvature| (the sharpest bend), restricted
# to the interior where the curve is well defined. The signed curvature is not
# used: its max sits on the nearly flat small-eps end and is not a corner.
valid = np.zeros_like(curv, dtype=bool)
valid[2:-2] = True
k = np.argmax(np.where(valid, np.abs(curv), -np.inf))
eps_corner = eps_grid[k]

# Discrepancy principle: eps where ||Ax-y||_2 equals the expected noise norm.
# Residual increases monotonically with eps, so interpolate in log-log space.
target = SIGMA * np.sqrt(M)
eps_disc = np.exp(np.interp(np.log(target), np.log(res_norm), np.log(eps_grid)))

print(f"{'eps':>10} {'||r||_2':>10} {'||x||_1':>10} {'nnz':>5} {'curv':>10}")
for i in range(eps_grid.size):
    mark = " <- corner" if i == k else ""
    print(f"{eps_grid[i]:10.4g} {res_norm[i]:10.4g} {l1_norm[i]:10.4g} "
          f"{nnz[i]:5d} {curv[i]:10.3g}{mark}")
print(f"expected residual norm sigma*sqrt(M) = {target:.4g}")
print(f"discrepancy-principle eps = {eps_disc:.4g}")
print(f"L-curve corner eps        = {eps_corner:.4g}")

try:
    import matplotlib
    matplotlib.use("Agg")
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

    fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(5, 4))
    ax.loglog(res_norm, l1_norm, "o-", ms=3)
    ax.loglog(res_norm[k], l1_norm[k], "r*", ms=14, label=f"corner eps={eps_corner:.3g}")
    ax.axvline(target, color="gray", ls="--", label="sigma*sqrt(M)")
    ax.set_xlabel(r"$\||Ax-y\|_2$")
    ax.set_ylabel(r"$\||x\|_1$")
    ax.legend()
    fig.tight_layout()
    fig.savefig("lcurve.png", dpi=120)
    print("saved lcurve.png")
except ImportError:
    print("matplotlib not available; skipped plot")

# Recommendation: the noise level is known (sigma = 0.15), so the discrepancy
# principle is the justified choice: it fits the data to the noise and no more.
# The L-curve corner is printed for reference; it lies at a much larger residual
# (overregularized relative to the known noise), so it is not used.
eps_chosen = eps_disc
print(f"residual at corner = {res_norm[k]:.4g} ({res_norm[k] / target:.2f} x noise norm)")
print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={eps_chosen:.4g}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I recommend **`eps = 1.16`**, taken from the L-curve corner. The script is saved as `sweep.py` in the current folder, and its last line is `EPS_CHOSEN=1.16302`.

**Method**

- I swept `eps` over 60 log-spaced values from 0.0021 to 1.65, with `eps_max = max|Aᵀy|` as the upper end where the solution is zero.
- Each solve ran FISTA with up to 4000 iterations and `tol=1e-14`, warm-started from the previous `eps`. The corner was then found as the point of maximum |curvature| of the curve (log residual norm, log L1 norm) against log `eps`.

**Result**

| eps | residual | L1 | nnz | true support found | spurious |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.576 | 1.29 | 7.08 | 14 | 6/6 | 8 |
| **1.163** | **1.82** | **5.16** | **7** | **6/6** | **1** |
| 2.0 | — | — | 5 | 5/6 | 0 |

The corner is at `eps = 1.163`, with 7 non-zeros against 6 true. The curvature peak is broad: |κ| is about 0.32–0.53 across roughly 0.8 to 1.7, so anything in that range is close to equally good.

**Why this value**

- The L-curve corner is the method you asked for, and it gives a sparse solution with one spurious spike and all six true spikes recovered.
- The discrepancy principle (residual ≈ σ√M ≈ 1.34) would give `eps ≈ 0.58`. That is a valid cross-check, but it keeps 8 spurious spikes, so I did not use it.
- At 1.16 the residual (1.82) is above the noise level. That is expected for L1 shrinkage: the solution is biased toward zero, so the relative error is 0.45 versus 0.28 at 0.58. If you care more about amplitudes than support, use something nearer 0.6.

**Your `eps=2.0` run**

With converged solves, `eps=2.0` recovers only 5 of 6 true spikes. Your 4 non-zeros are probably a mix of too-large `eps` and the 500 iterations, which may not have converged. Iteration count matters here: I did not test `niter=500` directly.

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista

rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M = 200, 80
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
sigma = 0.15
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(M)

# eps at which the solution is exactly zero
eps_max = np.max(np.abs(A.T @ y))
eps_grid = eps_max * np.logspace(-3, 0, 60)[::-1]  # large -> small, for warm starts

res, l1, nnz = [], [], []
x = np.zeros(N)
for eps in eps_grid:
    x = fista(Op, y, eps=eps, x0=x, niter=4000, tol=1e-14)[0]
    res.append(np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x))
    l1.append(np.sum(np.abs(x)))
    nnz.append(np.sum(np.abs(x) > 1e-4))
res, l1, nnz = map(np.asarray, (res, l1, nnz))
order = np.argsort(eps_grid)  # ascending eps for the curve
eps_grid, res, l1, nnz = eps_grid[order], res[order], l1[order], nnz[order]

# L-curve corner: maximum |curvature| of (log residual, log L1) vs log eps
t = np.log(eps_grid)
u, v = np.log(res), np.log(l1)
du, dv = np.gradient(u, t), np.gradient(v, t)
d2u, d2v = np.gradient(du, t), np.gradient(dv, t)
kappa = (du * d2v - dv * d2u) / (du**2 + dv**2) ** 1.5
kappa[:2] = 0
kappa[-2:] = 0
i_corner = int(np.argmax(np.abs(kappa)))
eps_corner = eps_grid[i_corner]

# discrepancy principle cross-check: residual norm ~ sigma * sqrt(M)
target = sigma * np.sqrt(M)
ok = np.where(res <= target)[0]
i_disc = int(ok.max())  # largest eps whose residual stays below the noise level
eps_disc = eps_grid[i_disc]

print(f"{'eps':>10} {'resid':>10} {'L1':>10} {'nnz':>5} {'kappa':>10}")
for i in range(0, len(eps_grid), 3):
    print(f"{eps_grid[i]:10.4f} {res[i]:10.4f} {l1[i]:10.3f} {nnz[i]:5d} {kappa[i]:10.3f}")
print(f"true nnz = {np.sum(xtrue != 0)}, noise target sigma*sqrt(M) = {target:.4f}")
print(f"L-curve corner (max |curvature|): eps={eps_corner:.4f}, nnz={nnz[i_corner]}, resid={res[i_corner]:.4f}")
print(f"Discrepancy principle:            eps={eps_disc:.4f}, nnz={nnz[i_disc]}, resid={res[i_disc]:.4f}")
print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={eps_corner:.6g}")

I chose **`eps = 1.124`**, the point of maximum curvature on the L-curve. The script is at `/private/tmp/e-j12chZ/home/cwd/sweep.py`, and its last line is `EPS_CHOSEN=1.124`.

**What the script does:** it sweeps 60 log-spaced `eps` values from 1e-3 to 10 and runs `fista` (niter=3000, tol=1e-12) at each. It records the data residual ‖y − Ax‖₂ and the L1 norm ‖x‖₁, then takes the corner as the point of largest |curvature| of the log-log curve.

**Results:**
- Max-|curvature| corner: `eps = 1.124`, residual 1.79, L1 = 5.28, 7 non-zeros (true model has 6).
- At `eps = 2.0` (your run), the residual is about 2.6 and there are 5 non-zeros in this sweep. The corner is on the over-regularized side, so 2.0 is too large.
- Discrepancy-principle cross-check: the residual should be near σ√M = 0.15·√80 ≈ 1.34. That gives `eps ≈ 0.60`, with 11 non-zeros.

**Caveats:**
- The two methods disagree (1.12 vs 0.60). The curvature is noisy: it changes sign around eps ≈ 0.5 and 3, and has a second local peak near 0.2. I picked the curvature corner because you asked for the L-curve corner, not because it is clearly more reliable.
- At 1.124 the residual (1.79) is above the noise-level target (1.34). That is consistent with mild over-regularization, which is what you would expect at the corner.
- The script also prints the relative error against the true model, as a diagnostic only. It is lowest (~0.24) around eps ≈ 0.25 and is 0.44 at 1.124. That gap is the usual L1 shrinkage bias on the amplitudes, not a sign that the support is wrong. If amplitude accuracy matters more than sparsity, a smaller eps or a debiasing step (least squares on the recovered support) is the better route.

"""L-curve sweep for choosing the FISTA L1 weight `eps`.

For each eps we solve  min_x 0.5*||y - A x||^2 + eps*||x||_1  with FISTA and record
  rho = ||y - A x_eps||_2   (data residual)
  eta = ||x_eps||_1         (L1 norm of the model)
The L-curve is the log-log plot of (rho, eta). Its corner is taken as the point of
maximum curvature of that curve. As a cross-check we also report the eps at which
the residual matches the discrepancy-principle target sigma*sqrt(M).
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista

rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M = 200, 80
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
sigma = 0.15
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(M)

# Sweep eps on a log grid. Stop when the model is empty (eta -> 0 makes log undefined).
eps_grid = np.logspace(-3, 1, 60)
rhos, etas, nnzs, epss, errs = [], [], [], [], []
for eps in eps_grid:
    x = fista(Op, y, eps=eps, niter=3000, tol=1e-12)[0]
    r = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x)
    e = np.linalg.norm(x, 1)
    if e < 1e-8:
        break
    rhos.append(r)
    etas.append(e)
    nnzs.append(int(np.sum(np.abs(x) > 1e-6)))
    errs.append(np.linalg.norm(x - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue))
    epss.append(eps)

rhos = np.asarray(rhos)
etas = np.asarray(etas)
epss = np.asarray(epss)
errs = np.asarray(errs)

# Max-curvature corner in log-log space, with derivatives taken with respect to log10(eps).
# The curve is parameterised by t = log10(eps); X = log10(rho), Y = log10(eta).
t = np.log10(epss)
X = np.log10(rhos)
Y = np.log10(etas)
X1, X2 = np.gradient(X, t), np.gradient(np.gradient(X, t), t)
Y1, Y2 = np.gradient(Y, t), np.gradient(np.gradient(Y, t), t)
kappa = (X1 * Y2 - X2 * Y1) / (X1**2 + Y1**2) ** 1.5
# The sign of kappa depends on the parameterisation direction, so the corner is the
# point of largest |kappa|. Ignore the endpoints, where finite differences are unreliable.
interior = np.arange(2, len(kappa) - 2)
k_idx = interior[np.argmax(np.abs(kappa[interior]))]
eps_corner = epss[k_idx]

# Discrepancy-principle cross-check: residual should be close to sigma*sqrt(M).
target = sigma * np.sqrt(M)
eps_discrep = epss[np.argmin(np.abs(rhos - target))]

print(f"noise sigma={sigma}, target residual sigma*sqrt(M)={target:.3f}")
print(f"{'eps':>10} {'residual':>10} {'L1 norm':>10} {'nnz':>5} {'kappa':>10} {'rel.err':>8}")
for i in range(len(epss)):
    print(f"{epss[i]:10.4g} {rhos[i]:10.4f} {etas[i]:10.4f} {nnzs[i]:5d} {kappa[i]:10.4f} {errs[i]:8.3f}")
print(f"max-|curvature| corner: eps={eps_corner:.4g} "
      f"(residual={rhos[k_idx]:.3f}, L1={etas[k_idx]:.3f}, nnz={nnzs[k_idx]}, rel.err={errs[k_idx]:.3f})")
print(f"discrepancy-principle eps: {eps_discrep:.4g} (residual={rhos[np.argmin(np.abs(rhos - target))]:.3f})")
print(f"true non-zeros: {np.count_nonzero(xtrue)}")

# Final choice: the L-curve corner. Print it as the last line.
print(f"EPS_CHOSEN={eps_corner:.4g}")

Prompt

I recover a sparse model with fista and I want to choose eps from the corner of the L-curve (data residual against the L1 norm of the model). With eps=2.0 I only get 4 non-zeros, and I would like a single justified number. The noise standard deviation is 0.15. Please build the L-curve and tell me the corner. My test problem:

import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
N, M = 200, 80
A = rng.standard_normal((M, N)) / np.sqrt(M)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
xtrue = np.zeros(N)
xtrue[rng.choice(N, 6, replace=False)] = rng.uniform(1, 3, 6) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 6)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.15 * rng.standard_normal(M)

xinv = pylops.optimization.sparsity.fista(Op, y, eps=2.0, niter=500)[0]

Please write a runnable Python script saved as sweep.py in the current folder, run it, and tell me what you chose and why. The script must print, as its last line, EPS_CHOSEN=<value> with the value you recommend.
